A. Modifications to Gas Tariff Provisions
10 
Q.
Please describe the current mid-year adjustment to the annual cost of 
11 
gas imbalance. 
12 
A.
The Company currently has the flexibility to implement an adjustment to 
13 
the annual cost of gas imbalance surcharge or refund as necessary, each 
14 
March 1 for the remainder of the calendar year for sales customers taking 
15 
service under SCs 1, 2, 3, 9, 15, 16, and 17.  If implemented, the adjustment 
16 
includes an adjustment for principal and interest estimated to be recovered 
17 
under the currently effective annual cost of gas imbalance surcharge or 
18 
refund during January and February of the year the imbalance is being 
19 
surcharged or refunded.  
20 
21 
2772


--- Page 153 ---
Q.
Why is the Company proposing to remove this tariff provision? 
1 
A.
The Company currently has the flexibility to implement a monthly cost of 
2 
gas imbalance surcharge or refund as needed from October 1 through July 
3 
31 to minimize any year-end gas cost over or under recoveries.  There is 
4 
currently no cap on the monthly imbalance surcharge or refund.  The 
5 
monthly imbalance provision provides enough flexibility for the Company 
6 
to manage annual cost of gas imbalances, inclusive of the prior year cost of 
7 
gas imbalances.  The calculation of a revised prior year annual imbalance is 
8 
cumbersome and is not necessary to manage customers’ cost of gas 
9 
imbalances.  For this reason, the Company is proposing to remove this 
10 
provision.   
11 
12 
Q.
Please describe the current mid-year adjustment to the MFC annual 
13 
imbalance. 
14 
A.
The Company currently has the flexibility to implement an adjustment to 
15 
the MFC imbalance surcharge or refund as necessary, each March 1 for the 
16 
remainder of the calendar year for firm sales customers taking service under 
17 
SCs 1, 2, 3, 9, 15, 16, and 17, firm transportation under SC 5, TC and IT 
18 
sales customers, and ESCOs that participate in the Company’s POR 
19 
program.  If implemented, the adjustment includes an adjustment for 
20 
principal and interest estimated to be recovered under the currently effective 
21 
2773


--- Page 154 ---
MFC imbalance surcharge or refund during January and February of the 
1 
year the imbalance is being surcharged or refunded.  
2 
3 
Q.
Why is the Company proposing to remove this tariff provision? 
4 
A.
The MFC imbalances are generally immaterial and the revisions to the 
5 
imbalances result in very small differences.  For the same reasons 
6 
mentioned above for the annual cost of gas imbalance, the Company is 
7 
proposing to remove this provision. 
8 
9 
Q.
Does the Company propose any changes to the current GSRS? 
10 
A.
Yes.  As explained in the GIOP testimony, the Company proposes to expand 
11 
the current GSRS mechanism to include a customer connections (unit cost) 
12 
productivity incentive.  In addition, the Company is proposing to include 
13 
clarifying language in the Tariff regarding the recovery of incentives 
14 
relative to leak prone pipe (“LPP”) removal, LPP productivity, and leak 
15 
repair incentives currently being recovered through the GSRS.  Finally, to 
16 
the extent professional engineering certification, operator qualification, and 
17 
similar pipeline safety requirements are finalized after rates are set in this 
18 
proceeding, the costs of those programs would be recovered through the 
19 
GSRS, and the Tariff would be revised accordingly. 
20 
21 
2774


--- Page 155 ---
Q.
What other changes to the Tariff provisions concerning the GSRS does 
1 
the Company propose?

3 
are charged the GSRS.  In the 2016 KEDLI Rate Case, SC 9 was 
4 
inadvertently excluded in the GSRS even though the surcharge should apply 
5

6

9

12

13

19

2

3

4

6

B. Modify the TC and IT Service Classifications

10

12

13 
the existing TC and IT services and to implement two pricing tiers 
14 
determined by the customer’s fuel switching equipment.  Additionally, the 
15 
TC/IT Order directed the Company to address related rate design issues in 
16 
its next rate case.  Accordingly, the Company developed SC 18 – Non-Firm 
17 
Demand Response Sales Service, under which it will serve all TC and IT 
18 
sales customers.  TC/IT transportation customers will be placed into a new 
19

20 
both SC 18 and SC 19, the Company proposes to implement two pricing 
21 
2776


--- Page 157 ---
tiers determined by a customer’s switching equipment.  As discussed 
1 
earlier, Tier One customers will utilize automatic switching equipment and 
2

3 
degrees Fahrenheit.  Under Tier Two, customers will utilize manual 
4 
switching equipment and receive service so long as the outside temperature 
5

6 
lower rate than Tier One customers due to the higher frequency of 
7

8 
classifications will continue to be subject to interruption for any supply or 
9

15 
response gas costs in its annual gas cost reconciliation.  Non-firm customers 
16 
will share in the annual over/under recoveries that currently only apply to 
17 
firm sales customers.

13

14 
or life-sustaining services (i.e., hospitals providing critical care, nursing 
15

20 
the steps the Company will take when critical care customers incur two 
21 
2779


--- Page 160 ---
unauthorized use violations (two strikes) in one winter season.  The

2

3

4 
customer’s expense; and (ii) the customer install, at the customer’s expense,

D. Green Gas Tariff
10 
Q.
Please explain the proposed Green Gas Tariff. 
11 
A.
As explained in the testimony of the Future of Heat Panel, KEDLI is 
12 
introducing an innovative RNG program that promotes renewable energy 
13 
source in its supply portfolio and offers firm customers the option of 
14 
purchasing RNG.  This program is available to all firm sales customers 
15 
served under SCs 1A, 1B, 1BDG, 2, 3, 9, 15, 16, and 17.  This offering 
16 
allows customers the opportunity to participate in the program by paying an 
17 
Green Gas Adder that will help to offset the premium cost for this renewable 
18 
product.  
19 
20 
2783


--- Page 164 ---
Q.
Please explain how customers will be able to participate in the Green
1 
Gas program.
2 
A.
Residential customers served under SC 1 will have the option to elect a fixed
3 
dollar amount to be dedicated to RNG in their monthly bill.  Non-residential
4 
customers will have the option of either a flat rate each month or a
5 
percentage of their monthly consumption be dedicated to RNG.
6 
7 
Q.
How will the Company reconcile RNG recoveries to actual RNG 
8 
procurement costs? 
9 
A.
The Company proposes to reconcile the costs of the Green Gas programs 
10 
separately from the non RNG procurement costs through a separate 
11 
reconciliation.  Any over or under recoveries will only be applicable to 
12 
customers participating in the Green Gas programs.
13 
14 
Q.
What tariff modifications will be necessary to implement the Green Gas 
15 
Tariff? 
16 
A.
The Company will add a “Green Gas RNG” rider to its General Information 
17 
section of the Tariff.  The Company will also modify its monthly cost of gas 
18 
section of the Tariff to include a “Green Cost of Gas” calculation.  
19 
20 
2784


--- Page 165 ---
E. Modifications to SC 9 NGV Tariff
1 
Q.
Please explain the proposed changes to SC 9 – NGV tariff provisions.
2 
A.
As explained in the Future of Heat testimony, NGVs provide one of the 
3 
most economically viable ways to reduce carbon emissions in 
4 
transportation.  As New York State seeks to decrease its overall greenhouse 
5 
gas emissions, transportation will be an important sector to address.  For 
6 
these reasons, the Company is proposing to design rates that will be more 
7 
closely aligned with the market for motor fuels used by fleets and to offer 
8 
an interruptibility option for new NGV customers on the SC 9 – 
9 
NGV rate.  This option will be offered to new NGV customers because they 
10 
are more likely to have options to temporarily modify their fueling profiles 
11 
during periods of peak demand.  The Company would install automatic fuel 
12 
switching equipment and interruptions would occur similarly to current TC 
13 
interruptions.   
14 
15 
Q.
How did the Company design the proposed SC 9 – NGV rates?
16 
A.
As explained above, the SC 9 class had returns significantly above the 
17 
tolerance band but was assigned a revenue target below pro forma revenue 
18 
at present rates because its current return is noticeably higher than any other 
19 
class and to support this energy efficient technology.  The Company is 
20 
proposing to increase the customer charge, which includes the first three 
21 
2785


--- Page 166 ---
therms, from $38 to $150.  For usage over three therms, the Company is 
1 
proposing a rate of $0.3743 per therm, which is a 42 percent decrease from 
2 
the current per therm charge of $0.6422 per therm.  This reduction in the 
3 
per therm rate was necessary to more closely align the rates with cost of 
4 
service and the market for motor fuels used by fleets.  As explained in the 
5 
Future of Heat Panel testimony, the Company proposes to monitor the 
6 
market and, if market conditions warrant, petition the Commission to adjust 
7 
the rates.  
8 
9 
Q.
Is the Company proposing to charge different rates for firm customer 
10 
and interruptible customers on this rate?
11 
A.
No.  The Company is proposing to charge the same delivery rates for 
12 
customers regardless of whether they are firm or interruptible.  The benefit 
13 
for interruptible customers is that they will save capital costs through a 
14 
lower contribution in aid of construction.  
15 
16 
F. Earnings Adjustment Mechanism Surcharge
17

18 
A.
As discussed in the testimony of the Future of Heat Panel, the Company 
19 
proposes a gas energy efficiency EAM to support the Commission’s REV 
20 
objectives and the New York State Energy Plan.  The Company is proposing 
21 
2786

to earn an incentive on these EAMs beginning in 2020 for the reasons 
1

2

3 
and collect on a per-therm basis.  Exhibit ___ (RDP-10) provides an 
4 
example of the calculated surcharge.  The EAM surcharge will be included 
5 
in the DRA recovered from SC 1, 2, 3, 9, 15, 16, and 17 firm sales and SC 
6 
5 firm transportation customers beginning the following April 1.

H. Paperless Billing Credit
16 
Q.        Please explain the current paperless billing credit.
17 
A.
The current paperless billing credit is $0.35 per service period for all 
18 
customers who receive their bill electronically.  The credit is based on 
19 
Historic Test Year costs adjusted for inflation and represents the difference 
20 
in cost to produce a paper bill versus producing an electronic bill.  The 
21 
2787


--- Page 168 ---
paperless billing credit consists primarily of the cost of paper, postage, and 
1 
envelopes offset by electronic billing costs.  This calculation is detailed in 
2 
Exhibit ___ (RDP-8), Schedule 2.

Is the Company proposing to change the current paperless billing 
5 
credit?  
6

7 
per service period.  The calculation is detailed in Exhibit ___ (RDP-8), 
8 
Schedule 2.

I.
Consolidated Billing Charge
11 
Q.
Please explain the current consolidated billing charge.
12 
A.
Currently, if the Company issues a bill for a sales customer, the customer is 
13 
charged $1.76 for each bill generated.  If the Company issues a consolidated 
14 
bill on behalf of an ESCO, the Company charges the ESCO a fee of $1.76 
15 
for each bill generated and the customer avoids the fee of $1.76 per bill.  
16 
17 
Q.
Is the Company proposing to change the current consolidated billing 
18 
charge?   
19 
A.
Yes.  As shown in Exhibit ___ (RDP-8), Schedule 3, the Company is 
20 
proposing to update the consolidated billing charge to $1.32 to reflect the 
21 
2788


--- Page 169 ---
results of the ECOSS.  The total revenue requirement for the Competitive 
1 
Billing function was based on a competitive function study and includes the 
2 
costs associated with bill printing and mailing and payment processing 
3 
reduced by the paperless billing adjustment.  The total adjusted revenue 
4 
requirement was then divided by the total number of gas bills to arrive at a 
5 
monthly billing cost of $1.32.

XIII.
Commitments from 2016 KEDLI Rate Case 
18 
A. Inclining Block Study
19 
Q.
Has the Company performed an inclining block study as required in 
20 
the 2016 KEDLI Rate Case? 
21 
2789


--- Page 170 ---
A.
Yes.  Pursuant to the “Order Adopting Terms of Joint Proposal and
1 
Establishing Gas Rate Plans” in the 2016 KEDLI Rate Case, the Company
2 
examined the effects of modifying the current residential rate design, which
3 
is a fixed monthly charge with declining block rates.  The Company
4 
examined four block designs: (i) the rate design proposed herein, which
5 
maintains the present ratio of tail block rate to mid-block rate (24 percent
6 
for SC 1A and 23 percent for SC 1B; (ii) reducing the slope (i.e., increasing
7 
the ratio of tail block to mid-block) by 50 percent; (iii) flat rates (i.e., tail
8 
block equals mid blocks, eliminating the blocked structure); and (iv)
9 
inclining blocks where the tail block is 125 percent of the mid-block.  The
10 
results of the study are shown in Exhibit ___ (RDP-11), Schedule 1.
11 
12 
Q.
Please summarize the results of the study. 
13 
A.
Exhibit ___ (RDP-11) shows that as declining block rates are eliminated 
14 
and the rate design moves toward inclining block rates, the changes in 
15 
delivery revenue from current rates becomes more pronounced.  For 
16 
example, for SC 1A, the Panel’s proposal produces increases ranging from 
17 
0.94 to 1.24 times the 12.97 percent increase for the average customer, and 
18 
the effects are progressive, increasing with increasing usage.  As the rate 
19 
design moves toward inclining rates, customers with either low or high 
20 
usage have increases while customers in the middle have decreases. 
21 
2790


--- Page 171 ---
1 
In addition, for SC 1B, the Panel’s proposal produces increases ranging 
2 
from 0.90 to 1.23 times the 6.92 percent increase for the average customer.  
3 
In this case, the increase in the tail block places an increasing burden on 
4 
higher usage customers, with inclining rates the average customer would 
5 
see a decrease of 16 percent while the higher usage customers would see an 
6 
increase of 46 percent. 
7 
8 
Q.
Please summarize the Panel’s conclusions from this study. 
9 
A.
The results illustrated by the study – widely divergent percentage increases 
10 
among customer groups within a class, and huge burdens on lower and/or 
11 
higher usage customers – are not acceptable from a ratemaking perspective.  
12 
The Panel notes that if the customer charge were maintained at the present 
13 
level, the effect for both SC 1A and SC 1B would be exacerbated. 
14 
15 
The Panel’s proposed rate design includes modest reductions in slope (i.e., 
16 
ratio of tail block to mid-block is increasing).  The Panel supports 
17 
continuing the modest changes it has proposed in this proceeding. 
18 
19 
B. ECOSS 100 Percent Demand
20 
Q.
Has the Company performed an ECOSS using 100 percent demand 
21 
2791


--- Page 172 ---
classification for mains?

4

0058 an initial study proposing segmentation of the SC 2 service classes for

21 
2792


--- Page 173 ---
indicate relatively large bill impacts for the SC 2 Non-Heat customers.  
1 
Also, as explained above, the Company is no longer proposing a revenue 
2 
per customer RDM for any service classification because a revenue per 
3 
class RDM better supports the Commission’s goals to promote cost-
4 
effective energy conservation, increased use of renewable resources, and 
5 
decreased usage of fossil fuels. 
6 
7 
XIV.
Gas Marginal Cost Study 
8 
Q.
Please provide an overview of the Gas Marginal Cost Study. 
9 
A.
The purpose of the MCS, presented in Exhibit ___ (RDP-9), is to compute 
10 
the incremental, or marginal, cost to the utility to provide the next unit of 
11 
service (i.e., the next dekatherm of design-day demand or the next customer 
12 
connected).  The Company is presenting an MCS for distribution demand 
13 
costs only; customer costs and commodity costs are not included.  As shown 
14 
on Exhibit ___ (RDP-9), Schedule 1, the proposed Marginal Delivery Cost 
15 
is $1.01 per dth.  Customers participating in the Company’s Excelsior Jobs 
16 
Program (“EJP”) will pay the marginal delivery cost rate for all gas 
17 
consumption in excess of the consumption included in the applicable 
18 
minimum charge.  Based on the Company’s proposal to move ETIP costs 
19 
into base rates, EJP customers will also be charged an ETIP rate in addition 
20 
to the marginal rate so that they continue to be eligible to participate in 
21 
2793


--- Page 174 ---
Company-sponsored energy efficiency programs. 
1 
2 
Q.
What are the components of marginal costs in the MCS? 
3 
A.
The MCS includes the capital cost of the assets needed to serve an additional 
4 
unit of design-day demand, as well as the O&M costs related to the assets. 
5 
6 
The initial capital cost is developed by quantifying the relationship between 
7 
capital spending for growth, and the growth (additional dekatherms 
8 
delivered) supported.  The initial capital cost is converted to an annual cost, 
9 
representing the amount that would be included in the annual revenue 
10 
requirement to reflect return on rate base plus associated income tax, 
11 
depreciation expense, property tax and insurance. 
12 
13 
In addition to the annualized capital cost and the associated O&M costs, the 
14 
marginal cost includes property tax and insurance costs related to the assets. 
15 
16 
Marginal Demand Cost is stated in dollars per delivered dekatherm. 
17 
18 
XV.
Levelized Rate Increases 
19 
Q.
Does the Company support levelizing the proposed rate increases over 
20 
term of a potential multi-year rate plan? 
21 
2794


--- Page 175 ---
A.
Yes. To ease the impact on customer bills, the Company will consider
1 
options to phase-in the proposed revenue increases over the term of a multi-
2 
year plan.
3

Q.
Does this conclude the Panel’s testimony? 
5 
A.
Yes, it does.

Introduction

Revenue Forecast
5 
Q.
Did the Panel update the Companies’ forecast Rate Year revenue?
6 
A.
Consistent with Department of Public Service Staff’s (“Staff”) position, the
7 
Companies updated KEDNY and KEDLI’s respective Rate Year revenue
8 
forecasts assuming that the NESE project will not be available during the Rate
9 
Year.  Accordingly, Exhibits __ (RDP-2AR) and (RDP-2BR) provide the
10

11 
using the sales forecast submitted in the Companies’ response to IR DPS-798,
12 
which updates the Companies’ sales forecast submitted in the corrections and
13 
updates filing to reflect a no-NESE scenario.  In addition, the Companies
14 
updated the LPC Revenue and Billing fees to reflect the sales forecast
15 
provided in IR DPS-798 and updated the gas commodity revenue forecast
16 
based on the NYMEX forward gas prices as of August 28, 2019.

Q.

1 
calculate the forecast Rate Year revenue? 
2

3 
revenue.  The SRP ’s model prices out Rate Year revenue at the service class 
4 
level.  In addition, the SRP used a different methodology to normalize the 
5 
historic data used in the ogive analysis. 
6 
7 
Q.
Do the Companies agree with the SRP’s revenue price out? 
8 
A.
No.  The Companies reviewed the exhibits provided in support of the revenue 
9 
forecast calculations included in the SRP’s testimony and noted a few 
10 
discrepancies.  The SRP’s revenue forecasts for both KEDNY and KEDLI

12 
of the sales forecasts provided in response to IR DPS-798, which included the 
13 
corrections and updates forecasts adjusted to assume the NESE project is not 
14 
available.  In addition, the Companies noted that the SC-4, SC-7, SC-12, and 
15 
SC-13 revenues were not included on Exhibit ___ (SRP-3) and were not 
16 
included in the revenue at present rates used by the Staff Revenue 
17 
Requirement Panel to calculate the incremental revenue requirement.  The 
18 
SRP corrected these issues and provided revised revenue forecasts and 
19 
revenue allocation exhibits in response to IR NG-02 as shown in Exhibit ___ 
20 
(RDP-19R).

1 
Q.
Do the Companies agree with the revised revenue provided by the SRP in 
2 
response to IR NG-2? 
3 
A.
No.  The Companies’ revenue forecasts using the sales and customer forecasts 
4 
provided in IR DPS-798 do not match the revenue forecasts provided by the 
5 
SRP in response to IR NG-2.  Because the Companies and the SRP are using 
6 
different models to forecast revenue, it is difficult to determine the differences 
7 
between the two calculations.  As explained above, the Companies’ models 
8 
are more detailed, and the historic data normalization methodology was 
9 
different.  The Companies support the revenue forecast methodology included 
10 
in Exhibit ___ (RDP-2AR) and Exhibit ___ (RDP-2BR). 
11 
12 
Q.
Do the Companies agree with pricing out the forecast of Rate Year 
13 
revenue at the service class level? 
14 
A.
No.  The Companies’ revenue and ogive models are designed to price out 
15 
revenue at the rate code level, which is then rolled up to the service class 
16 
level.  The Companies believe pricing out revenue by rate code is a more 
17 
accurate way to develop the ogive analysis and resulting revenue price out 
18 
because the rate codes that make up a service class could have different usage 
19 
by delivery block resulting in slightly different total revenue by service class.  
20 
21

Q.
What is the SRP’s recommendation regarding the Companies’ ogive 
1 
calculations? 
2 
A.
The SRP expressed concern over the raw billing data used for the ogive 
3 
analysis (at 15).  Specifically, the SRP noted that the normalization of the raw 
4 
billing data does not accurately account for canceled bills and rebills and 
5 
recommended (at 17) that the Companies revise the ogive calculations to 
6 
account for canceled bills and rebills.  The SRP asserted that the Companies 
7 
could achieve a more appropriate normalization of the data by ensuring that 
8 
the allocation to each block step is not above the maximum possible or below 
9 
the minimum possible allocation. 
10 
11 
Q.
Do the Companies accept the SRP’s recommendation concerning the 
12 
normalization of data used in the ogive calculation? 
13 
A.
The Companies agree to revise the normalization methodology but propose a 
14 
different methodology from the one that the SRP proposes.  The Companies 
15 
agree that only removing the negative values in the upper usage levels could 
16 
have an impact on the ogive calculation, although relatively immaterial. 
17 
Therefore, the ogive calculations used to the develop the forecast revenue in 
18 
Exhibit ___ (RDP-2AR) and Exhibit ___ (RDP-2BR) were revised to include 
19 
any negative values so that in effect, the Companies are including the full 
20 
2812


--- Page 193 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
Case 19-G-0310

impact of any cancels or rebills instead of potentially skewing the data by only 
1 
removing large negative volumes.

Q.
Please summarize the NYCRDP’s comments on the Companies’ ECOS 
1 
studies. 
2 
A.
The NYCRDP recommended (at 3) that the Commission accept the 
3 
Companies’ ECOS studies because they are reasonable and consistent with 
4 
past practice, and they allocate costs on the basis of cost causation.  The 
5 
NYCRDP also recommended that the Companies’ ECOS studies be accepted 
6 
as the primary guide for revenue allocation.  It also agreed (at 9) with the 
7 
classification of distribution mains as both demand-related and customer-
8 
related, as presented in the Companies’ ECOS studies. 
9 
10 
Q.
What is the SRP’s recommendation regarding the ECOS studies? 
11 
A.
The SRP recommends (at 27) using the historic ECOS studies provided in 
12 
response to IR DPS-504, instead of the Companies’ pro-forma ECOS studies 
13 
(using Rate Year information).  The SRP believes that using forward-looking 
14 
Rate Year information can introduce additional errors. 
15 
16 
Q.
Does the Panel agree? 
17 
A.
No.  The Panel believes that the ECOS studies should be prepared using the 
18 
costs, rate base, and physical units that are projected for the Rate Year, and for 
19 
which the revenue requirement, revenue allocation, and rate design will be set.  
20 
While the actual conditions in the Rate Year will be somewhat different from 
21 
2814


--- Page 195 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
Case 19-G-0310 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Rate Design Panel 
Page 9 of 50 
these projections, the same is also true of historic year data – the actual 
1 
conditions in the Rate Year will be different.  In particular, historic allocator 
2 
values may not reflect normal weather, and historic costs may be different in 
3 
amount and type from the costs the Companies will experience and the 
4 
amounts they will recover in rates. 
5 
6 
The SRP acknowledges that when “very large changes in utility operations are 
7 
projected,” forward-looking Rate Year data can be used.  The Panel notes that 
8 
the filing of a major rate case means that significant changes are projected, 
9 
and it is important that the ECOSS reflect, as closely as can be estimated, the 
10 
conditions that will occur in the Rate Year and the amounts to be recovered in 
11 
rates. 
12 
13 
The Panel further notes that the ECOSS is easily rerun to reflect the 
14 
Commission-approved final revenue requirement, thereby reflecting the actual 
15 
amounts to be recovered in rates.  The final revenue allocation and rate design 
16 
cannot be done until the final revenue requirement is known, and rerunning 
17 
the ECOSS does not delay this process. 
18 
19 
Finally, an important goal of revenue allocation is to move classes closer to 
20 
the system average rate of return.  It is not possible to measure whether a

IV. 
Revenue Allocation
4 
Q.
Please explain the Companies’ revenue allocation process. 
5 
A.
The Companies’ revenue allocation proposals move the returns produced by 
6 
each service class closer to unity (the system average rate of return) while 
7 
mitigating extreme impacts, a concept known as gradualism.  For both 
8 
Companies, a tolerance band of 25 percent was used to determine which 
9 
service classifications had a rate of return at present rates close to the overall 
10 
rate of return at present rates.  Classes with returns at present rates above the 
11 
tolerance band were given smaller percentage increases than the average 
12 
increase, and classes with returns below the tolerance were given larger 
13 
increases.  Some adjustments were made for outlier classes and to promote 
14 
certain goals (i.e., supporting interruptibility).   
15 
16 
Q.
Do the revenue allocation proposals presented in Exhibits ___ (RDP-
17 
4AR) and (RDP-4BR) follow the same approach as the Companies’ 
18 
proposed revenue allocation presented in the Panel’s direct and 
19 
20 
2817

Case 19-G-0310 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Rate Design Panel 
Page 12 of 50 
 corrections and updates testimonies? 
1 
A.
Yes.  As shown on Exhibits ___ (RDP-4AR) and (RDP-4BR), the percentage 
2 
increases for classes within, over, and under the tolerance band were adjusted 
3 
by a uniform percentage from the Companies’ initial April 30, 2019 filings to 
4 
produce the updated revenue requirement.  The one exception in Exhibit __ 
5 
(RDP-4BR) is that KEDLI’s non-firm service classification (which was 
6 
producing a return far above the average) was given no increase. 
7 
8 
For KEDNY, SC 1B was within the tolerance band and given a revenue target 
9 
increase of 12.85 percent over pro-forma revenue at present rates.  SC 2-1, SC 
10 
4A-HLF, SC 4A-CNG, and SC 4B were above the tolerance band and given 
11 
increases of 3.23 percent over pro-forma revenue at present rates.  SC 1A, SC 
12 
1B-DG, SC 2-2, SC 3, SC 7, and SC 21 were below the tolerance band and 
13 
assigned increases of 18.21 percent over pro-forma revenue at present rates. 
14

15 
classifications) were given decreases of 2.5 percent.  See Exhibit ___ (RDP-
16

Rebuttal Testimony of the Rate Design Panel 
Page 14 of 50 
The SRP agrees (at 30) with the Companies that classes within the 25 percent 
1 
tolerance band should receive increases at or close to the average increase. 
2 
However, the SRP proposed that classes with historic returns above the 
3 
tolerance band receive increases 20 percent lower than the average, and 
4 
classes with returns below the tolerance band receive increase 20 percent 
5 
greater than the average. 
6 
7 
Q.
Do the Companies agree with SRP’s recommendations on revenue 
8 
allocation? 
9 
A.
As discussed in more detail below, the Companies generally disagree with the 
10 
SRP’s recommendations because the SRP’s revenue allocation, which relies 
11 
on historic year ECOS studies, produces results that depart significantly from 
12 
cost-based ratemaking. 
13 
14 
Q.
Please comment on the SRP’s recommendation to use the historic ECOS 
15 
studies as a guide in revenue allocation. 
16 
A.
As discussed above, the Panel recommends the Commission reject the SRP’s 
17 
use of a historic ECOSS because it does not reflect the amounts to be 
18 
recovered in rates and does not allow parties to measure whether a proposed 
19 
revenue allocation moves classes closer to the system average rate of return. 
20 
21 
2820

Q.
Are the SRP’s adjustments to ETIP and the Tax Reform Surcredit 
1 
necessary if a pro-forma (Rate Year) ECOSS is used as the Companies 
2 
have done? 
3 
A.
No.  Those adjustments are not needed because Rate Year revenue at present 
4 
rates already reflects the roll-in of those amounts; the roll-in itself is supported 
5 
by the SRP.  Exhibits ___ (RDP-4AR) and (RDP-4BR), Schedule 1 
6 
demonstrates that the Companies’ revenue allocation proposals produce the 
7 
correct revenue. 
8 
9 
Q.
Please comment on the SRP’s recommendation regarding the increases 
10 
given to classes over and under the tolerance band. 
11 
A.
The SRP’s approach is flawed because it removes judgment from revenue 
12 
allocation and produces inconsistent results.  The SRP’s proposed increases, 
13 
especially for KEDLI, are inconsistent, because classes with similar returns at 
14 
present rates (for the Rate Year) receive very different increases.  In addition, 
15 
the SRP’s approach treats classes just within the tolerance band differently 
16 
from classes just over and treats classes just over the tolerance band the same 
17 
as classes significantly over.  Finally, because the SRP’s approach is based on 
18 
historic year data, it necessarily ignores a key measure of revenue allocation- 
19 
whether it results in classes making progress toward unity.  For these reasons, 
20 
2821


--- Page 202 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
Case 19-G-0310 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Rate Design Panel 
Page 16 of 50 
the Commission should reject the SRP’s formulaic approach over the 
1 
Companies’ more balanced approach. 
2 
3 
Q.
Did any of the SRP’s other recommendation affect revenue allocation? 
4 
A.
Yes.  The SRP proposed (at 48-49) that the rates for the non-firm demand 
5 
response service classes reflect total bills that are lower than the applicable 
6 
firm service classes by 30 percent (Tier 1) and 40 percent (Tier 2).   
7 
8 
Q.
Do the Companies agree with this recommendation? 
9 
A.
No.  The SRP’s proposal to compare total bills for the non-firm service class 
10 
and the applicable firm service classes would affect the non-firm sales 
11 
customers (for whom distribution rates are only a portion of the total bill) 
12 
much differently from TC/IT transportation customers (for whom distribution 
13 
rates are the entire bill) and would produce significantly different results 
14 
between non-firm sales and non-firm transportation customers.  The 
15 
Companies believe their proposed rate design provides for attractive price 
16 
signals for non-firm demand response service. 
17 
18 
Q.
Please summarize UIU’s comments regarding revenue allocation. 
19 
A.
UIU recommends (at 35) that revenue allocation be evaluated using both the 
20 
Companies’ ECOS studies (which use Rate Year data and a customer-demand 
21 
2822

3

Case 19-G-0309 
Case 19-G-0310 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Rate Design Panel 
Page 18 of 50 
Q 
Please comment on UIU’s recommendation that the Commission place 
1 
boundaries around the relative percentage change a class may receive. 
2 
A.
The Panel agrees that gradualism is an important goal of revenue allocation 
3 
and rate design, and explicitly reflected that in its proposals.  The Panel does 
4 
not believe, however, that any purpose would be served by hard and fast rules. 
5 
For example, if the overall increase is five percent, it may be reasonable for a 
6 
class to receive an increase of twice the average, or ten percent.  But if the 
7 
overall increase if 20 percent, it may create hardship for a class to receive an 
8 
increase of 1.5 times the average, or 30 percent.  The Panel supports the use of 
9 
boundaries that are developed in response to the specifics of each rate filing, 
10 
as reflected in the proposals it has offered in these rate cases.

V.
Rate Design
7 
Q.
Please briefly describe the Companies’ rate design presented in Exhibits
8 
___ (RDP-4AR) and (RDP-4BR), Schedule 3.
9 
A.
For both KEDNY and KEDLI, the proposed rate design includes volumetric
10 
rates that, together with the minimum charges, are designed to produce the
11 
revenue target for each class.  For the classes served by KEDNY and KEDLI
12 
with blocked rates, the rates reduce the slope (i.e., the ratio of mid-block rate
13 
to tail-block) compared to the present rate design.  A lower slope supports
14 
conservation by making the tail-block rate higher.  The Companies also
15 
submitted an inclining block study (Exhibit ___ (RDP-11)), which analyzed
16 
the bill impact of four rate design scenarios including an inclining block
17 
structure where the tail-block rate would be higher than the mid-block rate.
18 
The study showed that a significant reduction in slope would cause either
19 
regressive bill impacts (lower usage customers would see higher increases
20 
than higher usage customers) or a huge burden on higher usage customers.
21 
2825


--- Page 206 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
Case 19-G-0310 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Rate Design Panel 
Page 20 of 50 
Neither of these results is acceptable from a ratemaking perspective. 
1 
Therefore, the rate designs proposed by Panel in rebuttal include modest 
2 
reductions in slope and tail block rates lower than mid-block rates. 
3 
4

1 
VIII. 
Non-Firm SCs
2 
Q.
What costs are currently included in the gas costs for TC and IT sales 
3 
 
service? 
4 
A.
For IT sales service, the cost of gas includes the WACOG adjusted for LAUF, 
5 
a merchant function charge, and a Tax Reform Surcredit.  For TC sales 
6 
service, the cost of gas includes the same components, which are all 
7 
volumetric based on usage, plus an average fixed cost of gas demand charge. 
8 
9 
Q.
Do the Companies propose to change the calculation of gas costs for the 
10 
new non-firm demand response sales service class? 
11 
A.
Yes.  The Companies propose to use the current TC cost of gas formula for all 
12 
customers in the new non-firm demand response classes, so that all non-firm 
13 
customers will pay the demand charge.  The Companies develop demand 
14 
charges at the beginning of each GAC year based on a cost of service study 
15 
that allocates capacity costs to each service classification depending on that 
16 
class’s usage during the annual period, the winter period, and the peak day. 
17 
The demand charge is the one component that is currently not in the IT cost of 
18 
gas calculation.  
19 
20 
2833


--- Page 214 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
Case 19-G-0310 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Rate Design Panel 
Page 28 of 50 
Q.
Do the Companies agree with the SRP’s recommendation (at 50) to use 
1 
ICOG instead of WACOG in the gas cost calculation for non-firm 
2 
customers? 
3 
A.
No.  The Companies do not agree with this recommendation.  KEDNY and 
4 
KEDLI’s combined portfolios include having gas available for TC and IT 
5 
sales customers down to 15 degrees Fahrenheit.  The Companies manage the 
6 
KEDNY and KEDLI supply portfolios on a combined basis per Case 05-G-
7 
0903 to maximize supply and demand synergies.  Therefore, it would be 
8 
inequitable for any sales customers to pay only a portion of the supply 
9 
portfolio costs.  TC and IT gas costs have been calculated using the WACOG 
10 
since January 1, 2017.  The SRP states that the Companies have not created 
11 
adequate incentives to prevent non-firm customer from migrating to firm 
12 
service.  However, this recommendation would increase gas costs to non-firm 
13 
sales customers because the ICOG is almost always higher than the WACOG. 
14 
In addition, the SRP’s argument that firm customers are subsidizing non-firm 
15 
is not correct, because the times when the ICOG is likely to be set (e.g., the 
16 
coldest days) are the times when non-firm customers can expect to be 
17 
interrupted.  When this happens, non-firm customers have no cost 
18 
responsibility for setting the ICOG.  The main result of using ICOG instead of 
19 
WACOG would be to charge non-firm customers the costs they are trying to 
20 
avoid, reducing their incentive to take non-firm service. 
21 
2834


--- Page 215 ---

Case 19-G-0310

NYCRDP states that monetary penalty for so-called “second strike” 
19

20 
issued within five days of the end of a prior event and the customer can 
21 
2838

Case 19-G-0309

1

Customer Failure: 
8 
There is an amnesty clause available to Customers that experience an 
9 
equipment failure. Should a Customer fail to switch to its alternate fuel 
10 
when it is notified to do so because of equipment failure, the Customer 
11

18

20

21

Q.

1 
A.

2

3

4

5

6 
Safety Panel stated that volumes for inactive accounts should not be 
7 
considered LAUF gas and suggested that the Companies adjust the LAUF 
8 
calculation so that customers do not pay for these volumes. 
9

10 
establishment of LAUF targets and deadbands? 
11 
A.
The Companies agree with the SRP’s revised calculation of the KEDNY and 
12 
KEDLI deadbands, which align with the Staff White Paper recommendations 
13 
as explained in the SRP’s testimony.  However, the Companies do not agree 
14 
with the SRP’s proposal (at 58) to revise the KEDLI LAUF target to remove 
15 
2014 actual LAUF from the calculation.  The SRP believes that this amount is 
16 
an outlier, but the Staff White Paper recognizes that actual LAUF can vary 
17 
from year to year and for that reason a five-year average is recommended for 
18 
the establishment of each Company’s LAUF target.  In fact, the actual LAUF 
19 
in 2015 was the lowest LAUF percentage in the five-year average, indicating 
20 
that there are likely timing issues between years that are somewhat normalized 
21

Q.
Do the Companies agree with the SRP’s proposal to change the inactive 
1 
account adjustment from a volumetric adjustment to a financial 
2 
adjustment or that inactive account volumes should be removed from the 
3 
LAUF calculation? 
4 
A.

5 
does not agree that any inactive account adjustments for LAUF are necessary, 
6

7 
and to the extent that actual LAUF is above the upper deadband, the 
8 
Companies incur a penalty for inactive account usage.  For this reason, the 
9 
Companies believe that any adjustments to actual LAUF relative to inactive 
10 
accounts is duplicative.  The Companies calculated LAUF targets and 
11 
deadbands are included in Exhibit ___ (RDP-5AR) for KEDNY and Exhibit 
12 
___ (RDP-5BR) for KEDLI.

Case 19-G-0309 
Case 19-G-0310 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Rate Design Panel 
Page 41 of 50 
Q.
Do the Companies agree with the SESP’s recommendations? 
1 
A.
The Companies agree with the recommendation to continue to streamline the 
2 
tariffs and to develop a DRA statement.  The Companies disagree with the 
3 
proposal to perform a study to ensure that customers are in the correct service 
4 
classification and the proposal to recover non-labor O&M costs for demand 
5 
response programs in the DRA. 
6 
7 
Q.
Please explain why the Companies disagree with the recommendation to 
8 
perform a study to verify customers are on the correct service 
9 
classification? 
10 
A.
The Companies make every effort to initially place customers on the correct 
11 
service classification based on information provided by the customer at the 
12 
point in time that service is initiated.   The Companies inform customers to 
13 
fully and accurately fill out their applications to assist in placing the customer 
14 
on the correct rate.  If circumstances change that require a change to the 
15 
original service classification, the customer has a responsibility to notify the 
16 
Company to reclassify the account to the appropriate service classification.  In 
17 
addition, a study that reviews every account in the Companies service territory 
18 
would be extremely costly and time consuming and the Companies do not 
19 
believe significant issues exist that would warrant such an effort.  For these 
20 
reasons, the Companies disagree with this proposal. 
21 
2847


--- Page 228 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
Case 19-G-0310 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Rate Design Panel 
Page 42 of 50 
1 
Q.
Please explain why the Companies disagree with the SESP’s 
2 
recommendation to recover non-labor O&M costs for demand response 
3 
programs in the DRA instead of base rates? 
4 
A.
The Companies proposal is to recover the forecast non-labor O&M costs in 
5 
base rates.  If the intent of SESP’s proposal is for the actual non-labor O&M 
6 
costs to be included in the DRA and recovered on a lag, the Companies 
7 
disagree as that adds to the cash flow issues effecting the Companies credit 
8 
metrics, as discussed in the rebuttal testimony of Company Witness Jonathan 
9 
Cohen.  If SESP is proposing the Companies recover the forecast non-labor 
10 
O&M costs through the DRA, the Companies could agree to move the 
11 
recovery of these costs from base rates into the DRA.  Any over or under 
12 
recovery of the forecast costs would be either refunded to or recovered from 
13 
customers in a subsequent period.  
14

NGV
16

--- Page 229 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
Case 19-G-0310 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Rate Design Panel 
Page 43 of 50 
1 
Q.
Please explain the Companies’ proposal to add interruptibility for NGV 
2 
customers.   
3 
A.
The amount of interruption would be negotiable for each customer with 
4 
various choices, depending on our need and their fueling patterns.  The key 
5 
component of this program is that the customer would decide on their 
6 
interruptibility because they would save on capital costs.  It would be up to the 
7 
customer to decide if they have that flexibility since this is an optional 
8 
program.  The Companies understand that fleet operation and fueling patterns 
9 
vary and some customers can do this easily and some not at all.  It is purely an 
10 
economic choice for the customer, but they have several options: (i) fuel 
11 
storage on site, (ii) multiple smaller compressors, (iii) more fuel capacity 
12 
onboard, or (iv) use of public or shared fueling. 
13

Q.
Summarize the method the Companies used to 
24 
2924


--- Page 305 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
9
perform their forecasts. 
1 
A.
With reference to the response to IR DPS-608, 
2 
the Companies used version 3.5.3 of the “R” 
3 
statistical software package to perform their 
4 
forecasting.  The Companies aggregated similar 
5 
service classes (SCs) for both KEDNY and KEDLI 
6 
into six forecasting groups: Residential 
7 
Heating, Residential Non-Heating, Commercial, 
8 
Multi-Family, Temperature-Controlled, and Other.  
9 
The Companies used a script for each forecast 
10 
that guides the user throughout the selection of 
11 
econometric variables and coefficients by 
12 
calculating their correlation with historic 
13 
data.  Having determined the relationship 
14 
between the various econometric variables and 
15 
each of the six forecasting groups’ customer 
16 
count and use-per-customer, the Companies used 
17 
forecasted econometric data from Moody’s 
18 
economy.com, the U.S. Department of Energy, the 
19 
Energy Information Administration, the National 
20 
Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration, and the 
21 
National Weather Service to forecast the 
22 
customer count and use-per-customer, and 
23 
subsequently the volumetric usage, for each of 
24 
2925


--- Page 306 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
10
its six groups.  The Companies then allocated 
1 
their forecasted numbers to the rate codes in 
2 
each group by using either the proportional 
3 
ratio of the last historic data point for the 
4 
customer count, or the monthly proportional 
5 
ratios for the last historic year for the usage.  
6 
The Companies then aggregated the rate codes to 
7 
their respective service classes. 
8

What do the Companies mean by customer count, 
9 
specifically? 
10 
A.
The customer count is the number of active and 
11 
flowing meters.

13

14

15 
A.

16 
which reflect the effects of energy efficiency 
17 
programs.  The forecasts thus also included the 
18 
continuing trends of energy efficiency programs 
19 
in the future. 
20 
Q.

21

22 
A.
By aggregating the classes into groups, one can 
23 
create data sets large enough for statistical 
24 
2926

Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
11
modeling, while also smoothing out any 
1

Rate Year Revenues at Current Rates 
4 
Q.
Please describe how the Companies developed 
5 
their forecast of the base delivery revenues for 
6 
the Rate Year. 
7 
A.
For all firm sales SCs, the Companies developed 
8 
base delivery revenue forecasts using the 
9

10 
deliveries.  For firm SCs that have multiple 
11 
block rates, delivery volumes were allocated to 
12 
the usage blocks within each service class by 
13 
aggregating rate codes using an ogive analysis. 
14 
Q.
Did the Panel review the Companies’ process to 
15 
determine if it was reasonable? 
16 
A.
Yes, but we used a different approach and priced 
17 
out using SCs rather than using rate codes. 
18 
Q.
Why did the Panel group deliveries by SCs rather 
19 
than by rate codes? 
20 
A.
We grouped deliveries by SCs because it is a 
21 
more efficient way to forecast as there are 
22 
significantly less SCs than rate codes.  We do 
23 
not believe that forecasting sales by rate codes 
24 
2930


--- Page 311 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
15
would achieve any significant increase in 
1 
accuracy. 
2 
Q.
What is an ogive analysis? 
3 
A.
An ogive analysis uses historic bill frequencies 
4 
to develop a cumulative percentage distribution 
5 
for usage levels within a rate code.  This 
6 
cumulative distribution is then used to allocate 
7 
the forecast deliveries into the usage blocks 
8 
for each rate code, after which the deliveries 
9 
can be priced at the applicable rate for the 
10 
particular rate block. 
11 
Q.
Did the Companies provide the raw billing system 
12 
data used in the ogive analysis? 
13 
A.
Yes.  The Companies provided the raw billing 
14 
system data in response to IR DPS-440. 
15 
Q.
Does the Panel have any concerns about the 
16 
Companies’ ogive analysis? 
17 
A.
Yes.  The normalization of the raw billing data 
18 
does not accurately account for canceled bills 
19 
and re-bills.  According to the response to IR 
20 
DPS-760, the Companies only remove negative 
21 
values from the block reports that would result 
22 
in a negative block step.  The Companies did not 
23 
account for canceled bills or re-bills that 
24 
2931


--- Page 312 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
16
increased the usage in a block step beyond the 
1 
maximum or minimum amount that could possibly be 
2 
in the block step. 
3 
Q. 
Please explain. 
4 
A.
For example, a block step from a usage of one 
5 
therm to two therms with 100 customers ending 
6 
their usage in the block would have a minimum 
7 
usage allocated to the block of 100 therms and a 
8 
maximum allocation of 200 therms.  We provide 
9 
our analysis of the normalization of the raw 
10 
billing system data for KEDNY and KEDLI SC No. 
11 
1A, Residential Non-Heating using the Companies’ 
12 
methodology of removing negative step volumes, 
13 
in Exhibit__(SRP-2).  We noticed that the usage 
14 
allocation exceeded the maximum or minimum 
15 
possible block usage.  Then we calculated the 
16 
absolute value of the volumes that exceeded the 
17 
maximum or minimum allocations of the blocks to 
18 
determine the percentage of volumes, compared to 
19 
the total billing data in each historic calendar 
20 
year 2018 month, which was not allocated 
21 
properly.  The KEDNY SC No. 1A usage allocation, 
22 
compared to the total usage, had from 0.14 
23 
percent incorrectly allocated in May 2018 to 
24 
2932


--- Page 313 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
17
2.98 percent incorrectly allocated in June 2018.  
1 
The KEDLI SC No. 1A usage allocation, compared 
2 
to total usage, had from 0.03 percent 
3 
incorrectly allocated in August 2018 to 1.24 
4 
percent incorrectly allocated in October 2018. 
5 
Q.
Does the Panel have any recommendations 
6 
regarding the Companies’ ogive calculations? 
7 
A.
Yes.  We recommend that the Companies revise 
8 
their ogive calculations to account for canceled 
9 
bills and re-bills.  At a minimum, this can be 
10 
done by ensuring that the allocation to each 
11 
block step is not above the maximum possible or 
12 
below the minimum possible allocation.

17 
Depreciation 
18 
Q.
Please explain what “depreciation” is, as it is 
19 
used in ratemaking. 
20 
A.
Depreciation is a method of recovering capital 
21 
cost, less net salvage, related to plant in 
22 
service over the plant’s expected useful life. 
23 
Q.
What is a depreciation rate and expense? 
24 
2935


--- Page 316 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
20
A.
A depreciation rate is a percentage rate applied 
1 
to the gross plant in service, by account, to 
2 
determine the annual depreciation expense.  The 
3 
annual depreciation expense is the amount 
4 
depreciated in a given year and included in 
5 
rates for recovery from customers. 
6 
Q.
Did the Companies file depreciation studies in 
7 
these proceedings? 
8 
A.
No.  The Companies did not perform a 
9 
depreciation analysis on the majority of their 
10 
assets, as the last depreciation studies were 
11 
filed in Cases 16-G-0058 and 16-G-0059, when the 
12 
Companies’ last sought rate increases.  For most 
13 
of the Companies’ assets, circumstances would 
14 
not have changed in enough of a meaningful way 
15 
as to affect depreciation rates.  However, the 
16 
Companies have plans to retire of all their leak 
17 
prone pipe (LPP) assets within the next 20 years 
18 
and propose to reflect this in their 
19 
depreciation rates. 
20 
Q.
What assets do the Companies consider LPP? 
21 
A.
The Companies consider all mains 12 inches or 
22 
less in diameter that are unprotected steel 
23 
pipe, cast and wrought iron pipe, and Alydl-A 
24 
2936


--- Page 317 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
21
plastic pipe to be LPP.  The Companies also 
1 
consider bare steel, wrought iron, copper, 
2 
Alydl-A, and some high-density polyethylene 
3 
plastic services to be LPP. 
4 
Q.
What deprecation factors did the Companies 
5 
propose to modify? 
6 
A.
The Companies proposed to modify the LPP accrual 
7 
rates and the LPP net salvage rates.  Together, 
8 
these two factors increased the proposed annual 
9 
depreciation expense by approximately $27.5 
10 
million and $24.5 million for KEDNY and KEDLI, 
11 
respectively. 
12 
Q.
Over what period did the Companies propose to 
13

14 
A.
The Companies proposed to recover the costs of 
15 
their LPP assets over 20 years. 
16

How did the Companies develop this proposed LPP 
17

18 
A.
The Companies claim that a 20-year period is 
19 
reasonable because the Companies are proceeding 
20 
with a program to retire and replace the LPP 
21 
assets on an accelerated schedule over the next 
22 
20 years.  The Companies propose to fully 
23 
depreciate the LPP accounts in 20-years so that 
24

1 
Q.
Why does the Panel recommend the LPP assets be 
2 
depreciated over 30-years? 
3 
A.
One of the principles of depreciation is that it 
4 
is more equitable to have the customers that 
5 
benefit from plant bear the cost of that plant. 
6 
However, the reason these LPP assets are being 
7 
replaced early is due to safety and reliability 
8 
concerns.  The Panel, therefore, finds it 
9 
equitable that current and future customers 
10 
share the cost responsibility, even if they did 
11 
not benefit directly from the replaced LPP 
12 
assets themselves. 
13 
Q.
What does the Panel propose with regards to net 
14 
salvage? 
15 
A.
The Companies proposed using a three-year band 
16 
to reflect the most recent pipe retirement costs 
17 
as the most representative of the future costs 
18 
the Companies will incur as they replace their 
19 
LPP assets.  Referring to the response to IR 
20 
DPS-611, though it is true that the total cost 
21 
to retire LPP in New York City increased 
22 
significantly in 2018, there is not yet enough 
23 
evidence to believe that the costs to retire in 
24 
2940


--- Page 321 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
25
2018 are necessarily representative of future 
1 
costs, and a three-year band would only overly 
2 
emphasize the 2018 costs if it were an outlier.  
3 
Referring to Exhibit___(PMN-4), pg. 2, the cost 
4 
to retire per mile retired can vary 
5 
significantly year-to-year within accounts; for 
6 
example, for KEDNY Account 376.05 Mains – Cast 
7 
Iron, the cost to retire per mile was $293,280 
8 
in 2018, $75,395 in 2017, $1,725,960 in 2016, 
9 
and $14,652 in 2015.  For KEDLI Account 376.18 
10 
Mains – Plastic, the cost to retire per mile was 
11 
$71,364 in 2018, $17,807,549 in 2017, $688,636 
12 
in 2016, and $1,892,937 in 2015.  The high 
13 
annual variability in cost to retire per mile 
14 
makes it difficult to regard the average of an 
15 
overly narrow band of years as meaningful.  
16 
Accordingly, we recommend using a four-year band 
17 
instead, which, in addition to mitigating the 
18 
effects of any outlier years, would also have 
19 
the benefit of capturing all activity related to 
20 
the LPP Acceleration Program, which started in 
21 
2015 from the Order Instituting Proceeding for a 
22 
Recovery Mechanism to Accelerate the Replacement 
23 
of Leak Prone Pipe, issued on April 17, 2015 in

Q.
Please briefly explain how the Companies’ 
17

18 
A.
The Companies prepared the ECOS studies by 
19

20 
requirement and assigning it to or allocating it 
21 
among the rate classes.  The ECOS studies rely 
22 
on a specialized model, developed by the HSG 
23 
Group, Inc., that uses the traditional three-
24 
2942


--- Page 323 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
27
step process of functionalization, 
1 
classification, and class allocation. 
2 
Q.
How did the Companies use the results of the 
3 
ECOS studies? 
4 
A.
The Companies used the results of the ECOS 
5 
studies to guide revenue allocation and rate 
6 
design, and to establish components of the 
7 
Merchant Function Charge and Billing Charge. 
8 
Q.
Does the Panel agree with the Companies’ use the 
9 
ECOS studies results? 
10 
A.
Partially.  We would prefer the Companies use a 
11 
historical ECOS study to guide the revenue 
12 
allocation process.  A pro-forma study is 
13 
forward looking and can introduce additional 
14 
errors in the results.  It is more appropriate 
15 
to use the historic cost of service studies to 
16 
guide revenue allocation, unless very large 
17 
changes in utility operations are projected.  We 
18 
used the results of the historic ECOS studies 
19 
provided in the response to IR DPS-504 to guide 
20 
our revenue allocation and rate design process.

Paperless Billing Credit 
20 
Q.
What is the Paperless Billing Credit? 
21 
A.
The Paperless Billing Credit is a credit given 
22 
to customers who chose to receive paperless 
23 
bills instead of paper bills.  The credit equals 
24 
2950


--- Page 331 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
35
the difference in the cost of a paper bill per 
1 
customer and the cost of a paperless bill per 
2 
customer.  Currently, the paperless billing 
3 
credit is $0.49 for KEDNY and $0.35 for KEDLI. 
4 
Q.
What did the Companies’ propose? 
5 
A.
The Companies proposed to change their credit 
6 
from $0.49 to $0.44 for KEDNY, and from $0.35 to 
7 
$0.41 for KEDLI. 
8 
Q.
How did the Companies’ justify the changes? 
9 
A.
The Companies explained that they propose to 
10 
adjust the credits to account for inflation from 
11 
when they were last set through the Rate Year.  
12 
As the cost of paper bills is an order of 
13 
magnitude larger than the cost of paperless 
14 
bills, a percentage increase due to inflation 
15 
will cause a larger increase in the cost of 
16 
paper bills, resulting in a net increase in the 
17 
paperless billing credit. 
18 
Q.
Explain why KEDNY’s paperless billing credit is 
19 
decreasing if there is supposedly a net increase 
20 
due to inflation. 
21 
A.
KEDNY had previously filed a proposed tariff 
22 
amendment in Case 16-G-0059 to revise its 
23 
paperless billing credit, which it ultimately 
24 
2951


--- Page 332 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
36
withdrew.  This amendment would have reduced the 
1 
credit from $0.49 to $0.41 to reflect increased 
2 
costs for paperless billing due to an upgrade to 
3 
the KEDNY’s paperless billing system.  Had the 
4 
amendment not been withdrawn, KEDNY’s proposal 
5 
in the current proceedings would have increased 
6 
the credit from $0.41 to $0.44. 
7 
Q.
Does the Panel have any issues with the current 
8 
proposal in these proceedings? 
9 
A.
We have no issue with the Companies’ 
10

11 
KEDLI.  However, at this time, we do not have 
12 
enough actual data to confirm KEDNY’s proposed 
13 
rates. 
14 
Q.
What does the Panel recommend? 
15 
A.
We recommend waiting until we have a full year 
16 
of actual billing data on which to assess 
17 
KEDNY’s rates.  While the proposed rates seem 
18 
reasonable, as of the date this testimony is 
19

The Companies found that moving rate design 
8 
towards inclining blocks would produce bill 
9 
impacts above the average customer’s impact for 
10 
both low and high usage customers.  The average 
11 
customer’s bill would decrease, and the high 
12 
usage customer’s bill would progressively 
13 
increase with usage.  For example, if inclining 
14 
blocks were used for KEDNY’s SC No. 1B, the 
15 
average customer’s bill would decrease by 2.77 
16 
percent and a high usage customer’s bill would 
17 
increase by 49.4 percent. 
18 
Q.
What conclusions did the Companies draw from the 
19 
studies? 
20 
A.
The Companies concluded that the bill impacts to 
21 
customers within a class varied significantly 
22 
with high and low usage customers receiving 
23 
substantial increases compared to the average 
24 
2956


--- Page 337 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
41
use customers.  The Companies stated that the 
1 
significant bill impacts to higher and lower 
2 
usage customers are unacceptable from a 
3 
ratemaking perspective. 
4 
Q.
Does the Panel agree with the Companies’ 
5 
conclusions? 
6 
A.
Yes, however the revenue requirement also plays 
7 
a large factor in the resulting bill impacts.  
8 
We agree that the bill impacts would be 
9 
substantial if any dramatic changes in moving 
10 
towards inclining blocks were made in this case 
11 
at the Companies revenue requirement.  Due to 
12 
Staff’s overall recommended revenue 
13 
requirements, we recommend rate design changes 
14 
that move towards flattening the blocks while 
15 
sending customers a price signal to reduce gas 
16 
usage. 
17

Interruptible Rate Design 
1 
Q.
Is it important to have IT customers on the 
2 
distribution system? 
3 
A.
Yes.  Gas distribution systems are designed to 
4 
meet firm customers’ heating loads at peak 
5 
periods, which normally occurs mid-winter.  
6 
Therefore, these systems usually have unused 
7 
capacity during the remainder of the year.  IT 
8 
customers take advantage of this unused capacity 
9 
and utilize the gas distribution system during 
10 
non-peak periods, while agreeing to have their 
11 
gas service interrupted during periods when the 
12 
system is constrained. 
13 
Q.
How should a utility price rates for IT 
14 
customers? 
15 
A.
A utility should set IT rates less than firm 
16 
service rates because the service provided is 
17 
not a firm service, and the utility does not 
18 
have to upgrade its distribution system to 
19 
ensure that it can deliver gas to IT customers 
20 
during peak periods. 
21 
Q.
Should IT service be set below the otherwise 
22 
applicable fully bundled firm service? 
23 
A.
Yes.  IT customers are required to maintain dual 
24 
2961


--- Page 342 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
46
fuel capabilities, and the costs of any 
1 
alternative fuels should be considered, because 
2 
such costs are part of the financial calculation 
3 
such customers must make in deciding if it is 
4 
worthwhile to take IT service over firm service.  
5 
From a rate making perspective, any non-firm 
6 
revenues reduce the firm revenue requirement.  
7 
Therefore, IT rates should be set at a point 
8 
between rates that are low enough to make it 
9 
economically beneficial for customers to choose 
10 
IT service over firm service, and rates that are 
11 
high enough to ensure that firm customers are 
12 
maximizing the financial benefit that IT 
13 
customers provide.  At no time should rates to 
14 
IT customers be less than the actual costs the 
15 
utility incurs to serve them. 
16 
Q.
Do the Companies continue to have IT customers 
17 
convert to firm service? 
18 
A.
Yes.  According to the response to IR DPS-798, 
19 
as of February 2016, KEDNY and KEDLI had 3,138 
20 
and 200 IT customers, respectively.  As of 
21 
February 2019, KEDNY and KEDLI had 2,569 and 182 
22 
IT customers, a reduction of 18 percent and nine 
23 
percent, respectively.  According to the 
24 
2962


--- Page 343 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
47
response to IR DPS-714, the decrease in IT 
1 
customers has been driven by migration to firm 
2 
service. 
3 
Q.
What does the continued migration of IT 
4 
customers to firm service suggest to the Panel? 
5 
A.
It suggests that the IT service is not priced at 
6 
a level that incents customers to choose to 
7 
remain on IT service as opposed to migrating to 
8 
firm service. 
9 
Q.
How did the Companies’ design rates for IT 
10 
service? 
11 
A.
KEDNY proposed to consolidate its temperature 
12 
controlled and IT SCs into a single 
13 
classification with sales and transportation 
14 
rates, SC No. 22, each with two tiers.  KEDLI 
15 
proposed to consolidate its temperature 
16 
controlled and IT SCs into two classifications, 
17 
SC No. 18 for sales and SC No. 19 for 
18 
transportation, both of which would have two 
19 
tiers.  The Companies proposed that tier 1 
20 
customers be interrupted at 15 degrees 
21 
Fahrenheit and tier 2 customers be interrupted 
22 
at 20 degrees Fahrenheit.  KEDNY and KEDLI 
23 
proposed to set minimum charges of $375 and 
24 
2963

Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP

$450, respectively, for both IT tiers.  KEDNY 
1 
proposed a tier 1 rate of $0.3174 and a tier two 
2 
rate of $0.3015 or a five percent discount from 
3 
the tier 1 rates.  KEDLI proposed a tier 1 rate 
4 
of $0.2907 and a tier 2 rate of $0.2762, or a 
5 
five percent discount from the tier 1 rates. 
6 
Q.
Does the Panel agree with the Companies’ IT rate 
7 
design proposals? 
8 
A.
No.  The Companies’ present rates for IT 
9 
customers have not created adequate incentives 
10

The Companies’ proposed rates provide similarly 
12

13

14

service.

At what levels does the Panel recommend setting 
17

It is not possible to calculate the exact costs 
19

20

the applicable firm total bill for Tier 1 and a 
1

Why does the Panel recommend increasing the 
3

4

A larger discount should be provided to incent 
5 
customers to remain on or convert to IT service. 
6

7

Q.
How did the Companies propose to price the IT 
17 
sales customers’ cost of gas? 
18 
A.
The Companies proposed to use the current 
19 
Temperature Controlled customer cost of gas 
20 
calculation methodology to determine IT 
21 
customers cost of gas.  The IT commodity cost 
22 
would be calculated by multiplying the weighted 
23 
average cost of gas by the lost and unaccounted 
24 
3160
2965


--- Page 346 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
50
for gas factor plus the non-firm demand charge. 
1 
Q.
Should this process be modified? 
2 
A. 
Yes. 
3 
Q. 
Please explain. 
4 
A.
The costs associated with non-firm gas purchases 
5 
are included in the weighted average cost of gas 
6 
for all firm sales customers.  This pricing 
7 
methodology includes portions of the gas supply 
8 
portfolio, such as base load gas supplies and 
9 
storage gas, that are planned far in advance to 
10 
meet the reliability needs and on behalf of the 
11 
firm sales customers that are unable to procure 
12 
or plan for their own gas supply and capacity 
13 
needs.  Gas costs are reconciled to all firm 
14 
customers.  IT customers’ usage is incremental 
15 
load on the distribution system and there are 
16 
time periods when the Companies pay incremental 
17 
gas costs for IT sales customers.  Therefore, we 
18 
recommend that the IT customers be charged the 
19 
incremental cost of gas to eliminate any 
20 
subsidies by firm customers.

23 
Q.
What are IT penalties? 
24 
2966


--- Page 347 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
51
A.
In order to ensure the reliability of the 
1

2

3 
natural gas under certain conditions.  To that 
4 
end, the Companies’ tariffs include provisions 
5

6 
to an alternative fuel and penalizes those 
7 
customers for the unauthorized use of natural 
8 
gas.

10

11 
the Companies?

13

16

18

19

20 
A.

21

22 
of” to the “lower of” (1) two times the sum of 
23 
the market gas price plus the applicable IT or 
24 
2967

Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
52
TC transportation rate; or (2) nine times the 
1 
applicable IT sales rate. 
2 
Q.

3 
penalty structure? 
4 
A.
Yes, in the Order Approving Tariff Revisions and 
5 
Directing Further Tariff Filings (February 
6 
Order), issued on February 7, 2019 in Cases 16-
7 
G-0058 and 16-G-0059.  The February Order
8 
explained that the purpose of the penalty is to 
9 
ensure that non-firm customers do not choose to 
10 
use gas during an interruption and pay the 
11 
penalty, rather than use their alternative fuel 
12 
when they have access to it.  IT customers 
13 
jeopardize system reliability if they do not 
14

15 
Companies.  The Commission stated that the 
16

18

19 
weather periods due to capacity constraints.  
20 
Even when limited to the lesser of the two 
21 
alternative calculations, the penalty will 
22 
continue to provide a sufficient incentive to IT 
23 
customers to cease using gas when required.

2968

Lost And Unaccounted For Gas 
21 
Q.
What is lost and unaccounted for (LAUF) gas? 
22 
A.
LAUF gas refers to the disparity between the 
23 
amount of gas metered into a local distribution 
24 
2971


--- Page 352 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
56
company’s (LDC) system and the amount of gas 
1 
billed for, and metered out of, the LDC’s 
2 
system. 
3 
Q.
What is the LAUF incentive mechanism? 
4 
A.
The LAUF incentive mechanism holds LDCs 
5 
accountable for LAUF gas.  Before a LAUF 
6 
incentive mechanism was established, LDCs were 
7 
permitted full recovery of gas commodity 
8 
expenses, regardless of the disparity between 
9 
the amount of gas metered into the LDC’s system 
10 
and the amount of gas metered out of the LDC’s 
11 
system.  The LAUF incentive mechanism limits the 
12 
amount of gas expense that LDCs can recover as 
13 
an incentive to control losses. 
14 
Q.
Did the Companies propose changes to their LAUF 
15 
incentive mechanisms? 
16 
A.
Yes.  The Companies proposed four changes: (1) 
17 
update the fixed LAUF targets and associated 
18 
dead-bands; (2) have the new LAUF targets become 
19 
effective beginning the new GAC cycle, or 
20 
September 1, 2020; (3) remove the inactive 
21 
accounts adjustment; and, (4) modify the 
22 
calculation to gross up gas costs for LAUF gas. 
23 
Q.
Please explain the proposed change to the LAUF 
24 
2972


--- Page 353 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SRP 
57
targets and associated dead-bands. 
1 
A.
KEDNY and KEDLI proposed to use the actual 
2 
experience over the last five GAC periods ending 
3 
August 31, 2018 to set their LAUF targets.  The 
4 
Companies stated that the methodology is 
5 
consistent with the Staff Whitepaper on LAUF 
6 
Gas, a copy of which is provided in our 
7 
Exhibit___(SRP-8).  KEDNY proposed a LAUF target 
8 
of 2.432 percent with a standard deviation of 
9 
plus or minus 0.540 percent.  KEDNY proposed 
10 
dead-bands, of plus or minus two standard 
11 
deviations from the LAUF target, of 3.512 
12 
percent and 1.353 percent for the upper and 
13 
lower dead-bands, respectively.  KEDLI proposed 
14 
a LAUF target of 3.218 percent with a standard 
15 
deviation of plus or minus 1.166 percent.  KEDLI 
16 
also proposed dead-bands, of plus or minus two 
17 
standard deviations from the LAUF target, of 
18 
5.550 percent and 0.886 percent for the upper

and lower dead-bands, respectively.

23

2973

the standard deviations from the LAUF target

exceeds 0.5 percent, the standard deviation used

to set the dead-bands should be limited to 0.5 
3 
percent.  As stated above, KEDNY’s and KEDLI’s 
4 
proposed standard deviations are greater than 
5 
0.5 percent.

We recommend using the methodology set forth in

the Staff Whitepaper, adjusted for KEDLI to

remove 2014, with a standard deviation limit of 
11 
0.5 percent.

Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310

that were inactive 90 days or more during each

5

1

2

10

System Benefits Charge 
1 
Q.
What is the system benefits charge (SBC)? 
2 
A.
The SBC allows the Companies to recover costs 
3 
associated with clean energy activities 
4 
implemented by the New York State Energy 
5 
Research and Development Authority (NYSERDA) and 
6 
the Company’s energy efficiency programs.  The 
7 
SBC consists of two surcharges: the Clean Energy 
8 
Fund surcharge, which collects funds for the 
9 
NYSERDA-run clean energy programs, and the 
10 
Energy Efficiency Tracker Surcharge, which 
11 
recovers the Energy Efficiency Transition 
12 
Implementation Plan (ETIP) costs. 
13 
Q.
Did the Companies propose changes to their SBCs? 
14 
A.
Yes, the Companies proposed three changes.  The 
15 
Companies proposed to recover ETIP costs in base 
16 
rates so that the SBC would only include the 
17 
costs associated with Clean Energy Fund.  The 
18 
Companies proposed that ETIP costs be recovered 
19 
from the following baseload distributed 
20 
generation and non-firm demand response classes: 
21 
Nos. 21, 17-21, 22, and 18-22 for KEDNY and SC 
22 
Nos. 17, 5-17, 18, and 19 for KEDLI.  The 
23 
Companies also proposed to include any over or 
24 
2981

both KEDNY and KEDLI.  The Companies would 
3 
reconcile actual ETIP revenues from all other 
4 
service classifications that pay for the ETIP to 
5 
the amounts included in rates over the term of 
6 
the rate plan.  Any differences would be 
7 
refunded or recovered through the DRA, including

9 
of the rate plan and would be set forth on the

Q.
Explain why KEDNY’s forecasted customer count 
2

this caused an increase in total usage.
